, as the first-order formula
\phi_{R}
defines
R
.
∎

Having identified a hierarchy of negatable formulas, a natural question to ask is whether it is possible to extend the hierarchy in some way while keeping the negatability of the formulas. Let us now try to provide an answer to this question.

Recall that the
\Sigma^{1}_{1}
-translations for the disjunction
\vee
, the existential quantifier
\exists
and universal quantifier
\forall
as given in
[
27
]
are as follows:

1.

\tau_{\phi\vee\psi}(R)=\exists S\exists S^{\prime}(\tau_{\phi}(S)\wedge\tau_{\psi}(S^{\prime})\wedge\forall\vec{x}(R\vec{x}\to(S\vec{x}\vee S^{\prime}\vec{x})))
,

2.

\tau_{\exists x\phi(x,\vec{y})}(R)=\exists S(\tau_{\phi}(S)\wedge\forall\vec{y}(R\vec{y}\to\exists xSx\vec{y}))
,

3.

\tau_{\forall x\phi(x,\vec{y})}(R)=\exists S(\tau_{\phi}(S)\wedge\forall\vec{y}(R\vec{y}\to\forall xSx\vec{y}))
.

These translations can well be non-first-order sentences, and thus none of
\vee
,
\exists
and
\forall
preserve negatability. Nevertheless, in the literature there are other variants of these logical constants, under which the set of negatable formulas is closed, such as the disjunction
\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\geqslant$}}
(introduced in
[
1
]
, known in the literature by the name
intuitionistic disjunction
or
Boolean disjunction
), the (weak) existential quantifier
\exists^{1}
and the (weak) universal quantifier
\forall^{1}
(introduced in
[
20
]
) whose semantics are defined as follows:

1.

M\models_{X}\phi\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\geqslant$}}\psi
iff
M\models_{X}\phi
or
M\models_{X}\psi
.

2.

M\models_{X}\exists^{1}x\phi
iff
M\models_{X(a/x)}\phi
for some
a\in M
.

3.

M\models_{X}\forall^{1}x\phi
iff
M\models_{X(a/x)}\phi
for all
a\in M
.

Clearly, for all formulas
\phi
of
\mathcal{D}
or
\mathcal{I}
, all models
M
and all teams
X
,

M\models_{X}\phi\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\geqslant$}}\psi\iff(M,rel(X))\models\tau_{\phi}(R)\vee\tau_{\psi}(R),

M\models_{X}\exists^{1}x\phi\iff(M,rel(X))\models\exists x\tau_{\phi}(R)

and

M\models_{X}\forall^{1}x\phi\iff(M,rel(X))\models\forall x\tau_{\phi}(R).

In view of this,
Theorem

2.3
can be generalized to cover extensions of
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{I}
with these three logical constants, and these three logical constants are then definable in
\mathcal{D}
and in
\mathcal{I}

3
3

3

Moreover,
\exists^{1}
and
\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\geqslant$}}
are uniformly definable in
\mathcal{D}
and in
\mathcal{I}
, since
\exists^{1}x\phi\equiv\exists x(\mathop{=\!}(x)\wedge\phi)
and

\phi\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\geqslant$}}\psi\equiv\exists w\exists u(\mathop{=\!}(w)\wedge\mathop{=\!}(u)\wedge(w=u\,\vee\phi)\wedge(w\neq u\,\vee\psi))
, where
w,u
are fresh variables.
.
If
\tau_{\phi}(R)
and
\tau_{\psi}(R)
are both first-order, then the above three
\Sigma^{1}_{1}
-translations
\tau_{\phi}(R)\vee\tau_{\psi}(R)
,
\exists x\tau_{\phi}(R)
and
\forall x\tau_{\phi}(R)
are first-order as well. By
Theorem

3.3
, this shows that the logical constants
\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\geqslant$}}
,
\exists^{1}
and
\forall^{1}
preserve negatability. Furthermore, it is easy to see that

1.

\mathop{\dot{\sim}}(\phi\wedge\psi)\equiv\mathop{\dot{\sim}}\phi\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\geqslant$}}\mathop{\dot{\sim}}\psi
,
\mathop{\dot{\sim}}(\phi\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\geqslant$}}\psi)\equiv\mathop{\dot{\sim}}\phi\wedge\mathop{\dot{\sim}}\psi
,

2.

\mathop{\dot{\sim}}\exists^{1}x\phi\equiv\forall^{1}x\mathop{\dot{\sim}}\phi
and
\mathop{\dot{\sim}}\forall^{1}x\phi\equiv\exists^{1}x\mathop{\dot{\sim}}\phi
.

Without going into detail we remark that making use of the disjunction
\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\geqslant$}}
the extended system of
\mathcal{I}
can be applied to give a new formal proof of Arrow’s Impossibility Theorem
[
3
]
in social choice theory. In
[
24
]
the theorem is formulated as an entailment
\Gamma_{\textsf{Arrow}}\models\phi_{\textsf{dictator}}
in independence logic, where
\Gamma_{\textsf{Arrow}}
is a set of formulas expressing the conditions in Arrow’s Impossibility Theorem and
\phi_{\textsf{dictator}}
is a formula expressing the existence of a dictator. The formula
\phi_{\textsf{dictator}}
is of the form
\mathbin{{\large\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\geqslant$}}}_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}
, where
\phi_{i}
is a first-order formula expressing that voter
i
is a dictator (among
n
voters).
By what we just obtained, the formula
\phi_{\textsf{dictator}}
is negatable in
\mathcal{I}
and the Completeness Theorem guarantees that
\Gamma_{\textsf{Arrow}}\vdash_{\mathcal{I}}^{\ast}\phi_{\textsf{dictator}}
is derivable in our extended system.

6
Armstrong’s axioms and the Geiger-Paz-Pearl axioms

Dependence and independence atoms, being members of the hierarchy defined in the previous section (see
5.2
), are negatable in independence logic. Therefore, Armstrong’s Axioms
[
2
]
that characterize dependence atoms and the Geiger-Paz-Pearl axioms
[
9
]
that characterize independence atoms are derivable in our extended system of independence logic. In this section, we provide the derivations of these axioms in order to illustrate the power of our extended system.

Throughout this section we denote by
\vdash
the syntactic consequence relation associated with the extended system of
\mathcal{I}
defined in Section 4. The crucial rules from
[
11
]
that we will apply in our derivations are
\exists
I
,
\exists
E
, the usual rules for identity (
=
) and the following ones, where we write
\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y},\mathsf{z},\dots
for arbitrary sequences of variables:

\subseteq
Id

\mathsf{x}\subseteq\mathsf{x}

\mathsf{x}\subseteq\mathsf{y}

\mathsf{y}\subseteq\mathsf{z}

\subseteq
Trs

\mathsf{x}\subseteq\mathsf{z}

x_{1}\dots x_{n}\subseteq y_{1}\dots y_{n}

\subseteq
Pro

x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{k}}\subseteq y_{i_{1}}\dots y_{i_{k}}

\mathsf{y}\subseteq\mathsf{x}

\alpha(\mathsf{x})

\subseteq
Cmp

\alpha(\mathsf{y}/\mathsf{x})

where
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
.

where
\alpha
is first-order.

\mathsf{x}\perp_{\mathsf{z}}\mathsf{y}

\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{1}\mathsf{v}_{1}\mathsf{t}_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\mathsf{s}

\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}\mathsf{t}_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\mathsf{s}

\perp
E

\exists\mathsf{w}_{3}\mathsf{u}_{3}\mathsf{v}_{3}\mathsf{t}_{3}\big(\mathsf{w}_{3}\mathsf{u}_{3}\mathsf{v}_{3}\mathsf{t}_{3}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\mathsf{s}\wedge(\mathsf{v}_{1}=\mathsf{v}_{2}\to\mathsf{w}_{3}\mathsf{u}_{3}\mathsf{v}_{3}=\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2})\big)

where
\mathsf{v}_{1}=\mathsf{v}_{2}\to\mathsf{w}_{3}\mathsf{u}_{3}\mathsf{v}_{3}=\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}
is short for
\mathsf{v}_{1}\neq\mathsf{v}_{2}\vee\mathsf{w}_{3}\mathsf{u}_{3}\mathsf{v}_{3}=\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}
.

To simplify the derivations, we will also use the following handy (sound) weakening rule for inclusion atoms that was introduced essentially in
[
12
]
:

\mathsf{x}\subseteq\mathsf{y}

\subseteq
W

\exists\mathsf{w}(\mathsf{x}\mathsf{w}\subseteq\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z})

By applying the rule
\subseteq
W
we can easily derive a more general version of the
\perp
E
rule:

\mathsf{x}\mathsf{x}^{\prime}\perp_{\mathsf{z}}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{y}^{\prime}

\mathsf{w_{1}}\mathsf{v_{1}}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{z}

\mathsf{u_{2}}\mathsf{v_{2}}\subseteq\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}

\perp
E

\exists\mathsf{w_{3}}\mathsf{u_{3}}\mathsf{v}_{3}\mathsf{t}_{3}\big(\mathsf{w_{3}}\mathsf{u_{3}}\mathsf{v}_{3}\mathsf{t}_{3}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\mathsf{s}\wedge(\mathsf{v_{1}}=\mathsf{v_{2}}\to\mathsf{w_{3}}\mathsf{u_{3}}\mathsf{v}_{3}=\mathsf{w_{1}}\mathsf{u_{2}}\mathsf{v_{2}})\big)

We first derive Armstrong’s Axioms. To state these axioms in full generality, we now introduce a generalized version of dependence atoms, atoms
\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
that can have a sequence of variables in the last coordinate. The semantics of
\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
is defined as:

1.

M\models_{X}\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
iff for all
s,s^{\prime}\in X
, if
s(\mathsf{x})=s^{\prime}(\mathsf{x})
, then
s(\mathsf{y})=s^{\prime}(\mathsf{y})
.

Clearly,
\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\equiv\mathsf{y}\perp_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{y}
, and we thus interpret the dependence atom
\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
in
\mathcal{I}
as the independence atom
\mathsf{y}\perp_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{y}
.

Example 6.1
.

The following clauses, known as Armstrong’s Axioms
[
2
]
, are derivable in the extended system of
\mathcal{I}
.

(1)

\vdash\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x})

(2)

\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y},\mathsf{z})\vdash\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{x},\mathsf{z})

(3)

\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\vdash\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})

(4)

\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{z})\vdash\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y},\mathsf{z})

(5)

\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}),\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{z})\vdash\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{z})

Proof.

We only give the detailed derivation for item (5). By the rule
\mathop{\dot{\sim}}
​
E
, it suffices to derive
\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}),\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{z}),\mathop{\dot{\sim}}\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{z})\vdash\bot
, which,
by the translation given in
Theorem

5.3
(see also Formula (
5
)), is equivalent to

\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}),\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{z}),\exists\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{v}_{1}\exists\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}\big(\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{v}_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{z}\,\wedge\,\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{z}\,\wedge\,\mathsf{w}_{1}=\mathsf{w}_{2}\,\wedge\,\mathsf{v}_{1}\neq\mathsf{v}_{2}\big)\vdash\bot.

By
\subseteq
W
and
\subseteq
Pro
, it is sufficient to derive

\displaystyle\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}),\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{z}),

\displaystyle\exists\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{1}\mathsf{v}_{1}\exists\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}

\displaystyle\big(\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{1}\mathsf{v}_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\,\wedge\,\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\,\wedge\,\mathsf{w}_{1}=\mathsf{w}_{2}\,\wedge\,\mathsf{v}_{1}\neq\mathsf{v}_{2}\big)\vdash\bot.

Further, by
\exists
E
, it suffices to prove

\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y}),\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{z}),\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{1}\mathsf{v}_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z},\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\vdash\mathsf{w}_{1}=\mathsf{w}_{2}\to\mathsf{v}_{1}=\mathsf{v}_{2}.

(11)

First, note that since
\vdash\mathsf{y}=\mathsf{y}
, by
\subseteq
Cmp
we have

\mathsf{u}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{y}\mathsf{y}\vdash\mathsf{u}=\mathsf{u}^{\prime}.

(12)

Now, we derive

\displaystyle\mathsf{y}\perp_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{y},\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{1}\mathsf{v}_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z},\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}

\displaystyle\vdash~

\displaystyle\exists\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}\big(\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\wedge(\mathsf{w}_{1}=\mathsf{w}_{2}\to\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}=\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{2})\big)

(by the generalized
\perp
E
)

\displaystyle\vdash~

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{1}=\mathsf{w}_{2}\to\mathsf{u}_{1}=\mathsf{u}_{2}.

(by
\subseteq
Pro
and (
12
))

Similarly, we can also derive

\mathop{=\!}(\mathsf{y},\mathsf{z}),\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{1}\mathsf{v}_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z},\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\vdash\mathsf{u}_{1}=\mathsf{u}_{2}\to\mathsf{v}_{1}=\mathsf{v}_{2}.

Hence, (
11
) follows (from the usual rules for first-order formulas that the system of
[
11
]
contains).
∎

Next, we derive the Geiger-Paz-Pearl axioms as follows.

Example 6.2
.

The following clauses, known as the Geiger-Paz-Pearl axioms
[
9
]
, are derivable in the extended system of
\mathcal{I}
.

(1)

\mathsf{x}\perp\mathsf{y}~\vdash\mathsf{y}\perp\mathsf{x}

(2)

\mathsf{x}\perp\mathsf{y}~\vdash\mathsf{z}\perp\mathsf{y}
, where
\mathsf{z}
is a subsequence of
\mathsf{x}
.

(3)

\mathsf{x}\perp\mathsf{y}~\vdash\mathsf{u}\perp\mathsf{v}
, where
\mathsf{u}
and
\mathsf{v}
are permutations of
\mathsf{x}
and
\mathsf{y}
, respectively

(4)

\mathsf{x}\perp\mathsf{y},~\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\perp\mathsf{z}~\vdash\mathsf{x}\perp\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}
.

Proof.

We only give the detailed derivation for item (4). By the rule
\mathop{\dot{\sim}}
​
E
, it suffices to derive
\mathsf{x}\perp\mathsf{y},\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\perp\mathsf{z},\mathop{\dot{\sim}}(\mathsf{x}\perp\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z})\vdash\bot
. Instead of applying
Theorem

5.3
, we will now use the more succinct definition of
\mathop{\dot{\sim}}(\mathsf{x}\perp\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z})
as given in Equation (
6
), and the desired clause is then equivalent to

\begin{split}&\mathsf{x}\perp\mathsf{y},\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\perp\mathsf{z},\exists^{1}\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}(\mathsf{w}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\,\wedge\,\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\subseteq\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\,\wedge\,\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\neq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z})\vdash\bot.\end{split}

By
\exists
E
, it suffices to derive

\Gamma,\mathsf{x}\perp\mathsf{y},\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\perp\mathsf{z}\,,\mathsf{w}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\,,\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\subseteq\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\,,\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\neq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\vdash\bot,

(13)

where
\Gamma=\{\mathop{=\!}(o)\mid o\text{ is a variable from }\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\}
.

First, we derive by (the general version of)
\perp
E
that

\mathsf{x}\perp\mathsf{y},\,\mathsf{w}\subseteq\mathsf{x},\,\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\subseteq\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\vdash\exists\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{1}(\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\wedge\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{1}=\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}).

(14)

By
\perp
E
again, we derive

\mathsf{xy}\perp\mathsf{z},\,\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y},\,\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\subseteq\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\vdash\exists\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}(\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\wedge\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}=\mathsf{w}_{1}\mathsf{u}_{1}\mathsf{v}).

(15)

Putting (
14
) and (
15
) together, by
\exists
E
we obtain

\mathsf{x}\perp\mathsf{y},\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\perp\mathsf{z}\,,\mathsf{w}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\,,\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\subseteq\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\vdash\exists\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}(\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\wedge\mathsf{w}_{2}\mathsf{u}_{2}\mathsf{v}_{2}=\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v})\vdash\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}.

To derive (
13
) it is now sufficient to derive

\Gamma,\,\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z},\,\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\neq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\vdash\bot.

(16)

First, by
\subseteq
W
, we have
\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\subseteq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\vdash\exists\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\subseteq\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z})
. Moreover, by
\subseteq
Cmp
, we have
\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\subseteq\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z},\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\neq\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z}\vdash\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\neq\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}
. Putting these together, (
16
) is reduced, by
\exists
E
, to

\Gamma,\,\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\subseteq\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\mathsf{x}\mathsf{y}\mathsf{z},\,\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\neq\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\vdash\bot,

and by
\subseteq
Pro
, further to

\Gamma,\,\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v},\,\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\neq\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\vdash\bot.

(17)

Now, for an arbitrary variable
o
from the sequence
\mathsf{wuv}
, we derive the following:

\displaystyle o\perp o,\,o\subseteq o\,,\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}

\displaystyle\vdash\exists pq(pq\subseteq oo\wedge pq=oo^{\prime})

(by
\perp
E
)

\displaystyle\vdash o=o^{\prime}.

(by (
12
))

Since
\vdash o\subseteq o
holds by
\subseteq
Id
, we conclude that
o\perp o,\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\vdash o=o^{\prime}
for any variable
o
from
\mathsf{wuv}
. Hence, we obtain
\Gamma,\,\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}\vdash\mathsf{w}^{\prime}\mathsf{u}^{\prime}\mathsf{v}^{\prime}=\mathsf{w}\mathsf{u}\mathsf{v}
, from which (
17
) follows.
∎

7
Concluding remarks

In this paper, we have extended the systems of natural deduction of dependence and independence logic defined in
[
22
]
and
[
11
]
and obtained complete axiomatizations of the negatable consequences in these logics. We have also given a characterization of negatable formulas in
\mathcal{I}
and negatable sentences in
\mathcal{D}
. Determining whether a formula of
\mathcal{I}
or
\mathcal{D}
is negatable is an undecidable problem. Nevertheless, we identified an interesting class of negatable formulas, the Boolean and weak quantifier closure of the class of
\Sigma_{n,\mathsf{k}}^{R}
and
\Pi_{n,\mathsf{k}}^{R}
atoms. First-order formulas, dependence and independence atoms belong to this class. We also gave derivations of Armstrong’s axioms and the Geiger-Paz-Pearl axioms in our extended system of
\mathcal{I}
.

The results of this paper can be generalized in two directions. The first direction is to identify other negatable formulas than those in the Boolean and weak quantifier closure of the set of atoms from our hierarchy. The other direction is to analyze the
\Sigma_{n,\mathsf{k}}^{R}
and
\Pi_{n,\mathsf{k}}^{R}
atoms in more detail. As we saw in
5.2
, first-order formulas and the atoms of dependence and independence belong to the
\Pi_{1}
or
\Pi_{2}
level. It is easy to verify that
\Pi_{1,\mathsf{k}}^{R}
atoms (including first-order formulas and dependence atoms) are closed downward,
\Sigma_{1,\mathsf{k}}^{R}
atoms are closed upward, and
\Pi_{2,\mathsf{k}}^{R}
atoms (including inclusion atoms) are closed under unions. First-order logic extended with upward closed atoms is shown in
[
7
]
to be equivalent to first-order logic. Adding other such atoms to first-order logic results in many new logics that are less expressive than
\Sigma^{1}_{1}
or independence logic and possibly stronger than first-order logic. These logics are potentially interesting, because, among other properties, by the argument of this paper, the negatable consequences in these logics can in principle be effectively axiomatized.
